The M1-multigroup model aims to describe the global behavior of radiation within each spectral group
g
(see figure
4
). To this end,
\mathcal{G}
sets of radiative quantities associated with group
g
are introduced, including the energy
\mathrm{E}_{g}
, the flux
\overrightarrow{F_{g}}
, and the radiative pressure
\mathbbm{P}_{g}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{g}

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\mathrm{E}_{\nu}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(41)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{F_{g}}

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\overrightarrow{F_{\nu}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(42)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}_{g}

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\mathbbm{P}_{\nu}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(43)

Moreover, we define the total radiative energy
\mathrm{E}_{R}
, the total radiative flux
\overrightarrow{F_{R}}
, and the total radiative pressure
\mathbbm{P}_{R}
as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{R}

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{0}^{+\infty}\mathrm{E}_{\nu}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu=\sum_{g=1}^{\mathcal{G}}\mathrm{E}_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(44)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{F_{R}}

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{0}^{+\infty}\overrightarrow{F_{\nu}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu=\sum_{g=1}^{\mathcal{G}}\overrightarrow{F_{g}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(45)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}_{R}

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{0}^{+\infty}\mathbbm{P}_{\nu}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu=\sum_{g=1}^{\mathcal{G}}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(46)

By integrating the monochromatic radiative transfer equations (
30
) and (
31
) over the frequency interval
\left[\nu_{g-1/2}\,;\,\nu_{g+1/2}\right]
, one obtains the radiative transfer equations within group
g
:

\forall g\in\llbracket 1,\mathcal{G}\rrbracket,\penalty\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}_{g}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\overrightarrow{F_{g}}&=-cS^{0}_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}(c^{-2}\overrightarrow{F_{g}})+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\mathbbm{P}_{g}&=-\overrightarrow{S}_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\end{array}\right.

(47)

where
S^{0}_{g}
and
\overrightarrow{S}_{g}
are the source terms describing interactions between photons of each group
g
and matter. Their general expressions are:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g}

\displaystyle=-<\left(\eta_{\nu}(\overrightarrow{n})-\chi_{\nu}(\overrightarrow{n})I_{\nu}(\overrightarrow{n})\right)>_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(48)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S}_{g}

\displaystyle=-<\left(\eta_{\nu}(\overrightarrow{n})-\chi_{\nu}(\overrightarrow{n})I_{\nu}(\overrightarrow{n})\right)\overrightarrow{n}>_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(49)

Adopting the assumptions previously introduced regarding radiation–matter interactions, these terms can be written as:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{E,g}\mathrm{E}_{g}-\kappa_{P,g}a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T})\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(50)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S}^{i}_{g}

\displaystyle=\chi_{F,g}^{i}\overrightarrow{F_{g}}^{i}/c\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(51)

where
a_{R}
is the radiation constant and
\theta_{g}
represents, in a sense, the radiative temperature of group
g
at radiative equilibrium, and is given by:

\theta_{\mathrm{g}}(\mathrm{T})=\left(\frac{<B_{\nu}(\mathrm{T})>_{g}}{a_{R}}\right)^{1/4}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(52)

The opacities
\kappa_{E,g}
and
\chi_{F,g}^{i}
are energy- and flux-weighted opacities for group
g
and are defined as:

\displaystyle\kappa_{E,g}

\displaystyle=\left.\left[\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\kappa_{\nu}\mathrm{E}_{\nu}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\right]\middle/\left[\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\mathrm{E}_{\nu}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\right]\right.\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(53)

\displaystyle\chi_{F,g}^{i}

\displaystyle=\left.\left[\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\chi_{\nu}\overrightarrow{F_{\nu}}^{i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\right]\middle/\left[\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\overrightarrow{F_{\nu}}^{i}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\right]\right.\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(54)

It is clear that directly computing the mean opacities
\kappa_{E,g}
and
\chi_{F,g}^{i}
is difficult, as their evaluation requires solving the spectral moment equations (
30
) and (
31
), as well as precise knowledge of the spectral opacities of matter. This approach, in addition to being complex, would significantly increase the computational cost associated with solving the M1-multigroup model (
47
). To simplify this issue, Mihalas
[
120
]
proposed replacing
\kappa_{E,g}
and
\chi_{F,g}^{i}
with the Planck and Rosseland mean opacities,
\kappa_{P,g}
and
\kappa_{R,g}
, respectively. In the present framework, we assume that the fluid is in LTE, which implies that these opacities depend only on the temperature and density of the fluid.

This approximation is justified near the radiative equilibrium regime, where the radiative energy tends toward the Planck function (
\mathrm{E}_{\nu}\propto B_{\nu}(\mathrm{T})
), and where the radiative flux can be expressed as
\overrightarrow{F_{\nu}}\approx\left.c\middle/\chi_{\nu}\right.\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\mathbbm{P}_{\nu}\propto\left.\partial B_{\nu}(\mathrm{T})\middle/(\chi_{\nu}\partial\mathrm{T})\right.
. It is particularly well suited to optically thick media, in which radiation efficiently thermalizes with matter.

However, this approximation becomes less valid in optically thin regimes, where radiation is more directional and departs from radiative equilibrium. Nevertheless, within the M1-multigroup framework, the finer the spectral groups, the more the mean opacities converge toward the spectral opacities: one has
\kappa_{P,g},\penalty\ \kappa_{E,g}\rightarrow\kappa_{\nu}
and
\kappa_{R,g},\penalty\ \chi_{F,g}^{i}\rightarrow\chi_{\nu}
, which reduces the impact of this approximation. In this context, the source terms then take the following form:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{P,g}(\mathrm{E}_{g}-a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T}))\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(55)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S}^{i}_{g}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{R,g}\overrightarrow{F_{g}}^{i}/c\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(56)

It remains to define the closure relation for each group in order to solve the system. In the present approach, as before, radiation is assumed to be symmetric around the direction of the radiative flux
\overrightarrow{F_{g}}
within each group. Under this assumption, the closure relation takes a form analogous to that given in (
36
):

\mathbbm{P}_{g}=\mathbbm{D}_{g}\mathrm{E}_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(57)

where
\mathbbm{D}_{g}
is the
Eddington tensor
associated with group
g
and is given by
[
98
]
:

\mathbbm{D}_{g}=\frac{1-\chi_{g}}{2}\mathbbm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}+\frac{3\chi_{g}-1}{2}\frac{\overrightarrow{F_{g}}\otimes\overrightarrow{F_{g}}}{||\overrightarrow{F_{g}}||^{2}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(58)

where
\chi_{g}
denotes the
Eddington factor
associated to the group
g
. Its determination requires formulating an assumption on the shape of the specific intensity in order to render the problem mathematically tractable. In the following section, we present the developments that enable its numerical computation.

2.3
The Eddington factor

Within the framework of the M1-multigroup model, the objective is to determine an expression for the specific intensity that minimizes the radiative entropy, in order to close the system of equations for each group and to derive an expression for the Eddington factor. The radiative entropy density is given by
[
53
]
:

h_{R}(I)=\frac{2k_{B}\nu^{2}}{c^{3}}[n\ln{(n)}-(n+1)\ln{(n+1)}]\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(59)

where
n
is the occupation number, defined by
n=\frac{c^{2}}{2h\nu}I
. The total radiative entropy
H_{R}(I)
is then defined as

H_{R}(I)=\sum_{g=1}^{\mathcal{G}}<h_{R}(I)>_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(60)

As seen in equations (
41
) and (
42
), the specific intensity is related to the radiative energy and flux in each group. We therefore seek to retain only the specific intensity
\mathcal{I}_{\nu}
that minimizes the radiative entropy, i.e. the specific intensity that is the solution of the following minimization problem:

H_{R}(\mathcal{I}_{\nu})=\min_{I}\left\{H_{R}(I)\penalty\ |\penalty\ \forall g\in\llbracket 1,\mathcal{G}\rrbracket,\penalty\ <I>_{g}=\mathrm{E}_{R},\penalty\ <c\overrightarrow{n}I>_{g}=\overrightarrow{F_{g}}\right\}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(61)

It can be shown that the intensity satisfying this problem can be written as
[
165
]
:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu}=\sum_{g=1}^{\mathcal{G}}\mathbbm{1}_{g}(\nu)\penalty\ \mathcal{I}_{\nu,g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(62)

where
\mathbbm{1}_{g}(\nu):\mathbbm{R}^{+}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is the characteristic function, equal to
1
for frequencies
\nu
in
\left[\nu_{g-1/2}\,;\,\nu_{g+1/2}\right]
and
0
otherwise, and where
\mathcal{I}_{\nu,g}
denotes the specific intensity in group
g
, given by:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu,g}=\frac{2h\nu^{3}}{c^{2}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{h\nu}{k_{B}}\overrightarrow{m}_{g}\cdot\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{g}\right)-1\right]^{-1}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(63)

where
\overrightarrow{\alpha}_{g}=(\alpha_{0,g},\overrightarrow{\alpha_{1,g}})
are the Lagrange multipliers of group
g
associated with problem (
61
),
\overrightarrow{m}_{g}
is a four-vector defined as
\overrightarrow{m}_{g}=(1,\overrightarrow{n}_{g})
, and
\overrightarrow{n}_{g}
is the propagation direction of light in group
g
. By setting
\overrightarrow{\alpha_{1,g}}\cdot\overrightarrow{n}_{g}=\alpha_{0,g}\beta_{g}\cos(\theta_{g})
, the specific intensity in group
g
can also be written as:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu,g}=\frac{2h\nu^{3}}{c^{2}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{h\nu}{k_{B}}\alpha_{0,g}(1+\beta_{g}\cos(\theta_{g}))\right)-1\right]^{-1}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(64)

In this context,
\alpha_{0,g}
represents the inverse of a temperature and takes values in
\mathbbm{R}^{+}
, while
\beta_{g}
measures the degree of anisotropy of the radiation in group
g
, with values in the interval
\left]-1\,;\,1\right[
. It is important to note that if
\alpha_{0,g}
and
\beta_{g}
are identical for all groups (i.e. if
\forall g\in\llbracket 1,\mathcal{G}\rrbracket
,
\alpha_{0,g}=\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{g}=\beta
), one recovers the specific intensity of the M1-gray model.

However, unlike the M1-gray model, no general analytical formula exists for the Eddington factor
\chi_{g}
in this framework. Nevertheless, Turpault showed that, in the M1-multigroup model, for any physically admissible pair
(
\mathrm{E}_{g}
,
||\overrightarrow{F}_{g}||
)
, there exists a unique pair of Lagrange multipliers
(
\alpha_{0,g}
,
\beta_{g}
)

[
165
]
. Consequently, the Eddington factor can be computed numerically using a root-finding algorithm, such as the bisection–Newton method or a line-search method, described in Appendix
B
.
Chapter B
Numerical calculation of the radiative quantities
.

In the next section, I will analyze the behavior of the Eddington factor in the M1-multigroup model, in accordance with the study presented in the corresponding article
[
134
]
. In addition, in Section
6
.
6
Eddington factor in the M1-multigroup model
, I will present a method based on AI, developed during this thesis, that enables an efficient computation of
\chi_{g}
.

To begin with, let us examine the parameters that influence the Eddington factor in the M1-multigroup model. The radiative energy
\mathrm{E}_{g}
, the norm of the radiative flux
||\overrightarrow{F_{g}}||
, and the radiative pressure component
\mathrm{P}_{g}=\chi_{g}\mathrm{E}_{g}
in the direction of the radiative flux can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{g}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\int\nolimits_{4\pi}\mathcal{I}_{\nu}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\Omega\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(65)

\displaystyle\mathpunct{|}|\overrightarrow{F_{g}}||

\displaystyle=\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\int\nolimits_{4\pi}\mathcal{I}_{\nu}\penalty\ \cos(\theta)\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\Omega\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(66)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{g}\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\int\nolimits_{4\pi}\mathcal{I}_{\nu}\penalty\ \cos^{2}(\theta)\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\Omega\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(67)

or, equivalently, by noting that
\mathrm{d}\Omega=\sin(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\phi
, setting
\mu=\cos(\theta)
, and using the expression of the specific intensity in group
g
, one can write:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{g}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle=\frac{4h\pi}{c^{3}}\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\nu^{3}\left[e^{\frac{h\nu}{k_{B}}\alpha_{0,g}(1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(68)

\displaystyle\mathpunct{|}|\overrightarrow{F_{g}}||

\displaystyle=\frac{4h\pi}{c^{2}}\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\nu^{3}\mu\left[e^{\frac{h\nu}{k_{B}}\alpha_{0,g}(1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(69)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{g}\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle=\frac{4h\pi}{c^{3}}\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\nu^{3}\mu^{2}\left[e^{\frac{h\nu}{k_{B}}\alpha_{0,g}(1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(70)

It is then possible to distinguish four particular cases depending on the spectral bounds of the group under consideration:

1.

\bm{\nu_{g-1/2}=0}
and
\bm{\nu_{g+1/2}=+\infty}
:
this corresponds to the M1-gray model, in which the Eddington factor
\chi_{R}
admits an analytical expression
[
132
]
:

\chi_{R}=\frac{3+4\penalty\ \mathrm{f}_{R}^{\penalty\ 2}}{5+2\sqrt{4-3\penalty\ \mathrm{f}_{R}^{\penalty\ 2}}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(71)

where
\mathrm{f}_{R}
is the reduced flux, defined as
\mathrm{f}_{R}=||\overrightarrow{F_{R}}||/(c\penalty\ \mathrm{E}_{R})
, with
\overrightarrow{F_{R}}
and
\mathrm{E}_{R}
denoting the radiative flux and radiative energy integrated over the entire electromagnetic spectrum, respectively;

2.

\bm{\nu_{g-1/2}=0}
and
\bm{\nu_{g+1/2}<+\infty}
:
Eddington factor for the first group;

3.

\bm{\nu_{g-1/2}>0}
and
\bm{\nu_{g+1/2}=+\infty}
:
Eddington factor for the last group;

4.

\bm{\nu_{g-1/2}>0}
and
\bm{\nu_{g+1/2}<+\infty}
:
general Eddington factor for all remaining groups.

In the following sections, I will analyze the parameters that influence the Eddington factor in each of these cases.

❖ Case
\bm{\nu_{g-1/2}>0}
and
\bm{\nu_{g+1/2}<\infty}

In this case, it is possible to perform the change of variable
\tilde{\nu}=\nu/\nu_{m,g}
, where
\nu_{m,g}
is the geometric mean of the frequency bounds of the group (
\nu_{m,g}=\sqrt{\nu_{g-1/2}\penalty\ \nu_{g+1/2}}
), in integrals (
68
), (
69
), and (
70
). The radiative quantities can then be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{k_{B}^{4}\penalty\ \mathrm{E}_{g}}{h^{4}\penalty\ \nu_{m,g}^{4}}

\displaystyle=a_{R}\frac{15}{\pi^{4}}\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\frac{k_{B}^{4}\penalty\ ||\overrightarrow{F_{g}}||}{h^{4}\penalty\ \nu_{m,g}^{4}}

\displaystyle=a_{R}c\frac{15}{\pi^{4}}\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}(1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\frac{k_{B}^{4}\penalty\ \mathrm{P}_{g}}{h^{4}\penalty\ \nu_{m,g}^{4}}

\displaystyle=a_{R}\frac{15}{\pi^{4}}\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu^{2}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

where
\delta_{g}=\nu_{g-1/2}/\nu_{g+1/2}
is a parameter taking values in
\left]0\,;\,1\right[
, referred to as the
group narrowness
, and
\widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}=h\nu_{m,g}\alpha_{0,g}/k_{B}
is the Lagrange multiplier
\alpha_{0,g}
made dimensionless using the frequency
\nu_{m,g}
. In other words, by introducing the reduced flux
\mathrm{f}_{g}=||\overrightarrow{F_{g}}||/(c\mathrm{E}_{g})
and the Eddington factor
\chi_{g}=\mathrm{P}_{g}/\mathrm{E}_{g}
, one can write:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{g}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{15}{\pi^{4}}\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}\right)^{1/4}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(72)

\displaystyle\mathrm{f}_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}{\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(73)

\displaystyle\chi_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu^{2}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}{\int\nolimits_{\delta_{g}^{1/2}}^{\delta_{g}^{-1/2}}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(74)

where
\mathcal{T}_{g}=k_{B}\mathrm{T}_{g}/(h\nu_{m,g})
is the dimensionless radiative temperature and
\mathrm{T}_{g}=(\mathrm{E}_{g}/a_{R})^{1/4}
is the radiative temperature of group
g
. As for any physically admissible pair
(
\mathrm{E}_{g}
,
||\overrightarrow{F_{g}}||
)
, there exists a unique pair of Lagrange multipliers
(
\alpha_{0,g}
,
\beta_{g}
)
. The equations above show that, for any pair
(
\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
\mathrm{f}_{g}
)
, there exists a unique pair of Lagrange multipliers
(
\alpha_{0,g}
,
\beta_{g}
)
, which then allows the Eddington factor to be computed. One therefore concludes that, in the present case, the Eddington factor depends only on three parameters:

Figure 5
:
Evolution of the Eddington factor
\chi_{g}
at different radiative temperatures
\mathcal{T}_{g}
, using a group narrowness
\delta_{g}=10^{-4}
.

1.

The dimensionless radiative temperature
\bm{\mathcal{T}_{g}}
:
comparison between the equivalent radiative energy of the group,
k_{B}\mathrm{T}_{g}
, and the geometric mean of the photon energy levels within the group,
h\nu_{m,g}
;

2.

The reduced flux
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{f}_{g}}
:
degree of anisotropy of the radiation within group
g
. Its values lie in
\left[0\,;\,1\right[
(
\mathrm{f}_{g}=0
: isotropic radiation,
\mathrm{f}_{g}\rightarrow 1
: highly directional radiation);

3.

The group narrowness
\bm{\delta_{g}}
:
indicator of the separation between the frequency bounds of the group,
\nu_{g-1/2}
and
\nu_{g+1/2}
, defined by the relation
\delta_{g}=\nu_{g-1/2}/\nu_{g+1/2}
, with values in the interval
\left]0\,;\,1\right[
(
\delta_{g}\rightarrow 0
: widely separated frequency bounds,
\delta_{g}\rightarrow 1
: very close frequency bounds).

In order to gain insight into the dependence of the Eddington factor on the reduced flux, I plotted its evolution for different values of the dimensionless radiative temperature in figure
5
. Two common features can be observed across all curves:

1.

For any value of
\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
\chi_{g}
always varies from
1/3
at
\mathrm{f}_{g}=0
to
1
at
\mathrm{f}_{g}=1
, and has a zero slope at
\mathrm{f}_{g}=0
;

2.

\mathcal{T}_{g}
influences the evolution of
\chi_{g}
only at intermediate values of
\mathrm{f}_{g}
.

Overall, the Eddington factor depends more strongly on the reduced flux
\mathrm{f}_{g}
than on the radiative temperature and the group narowness. However, this dependence does exist, and neglecting it leads to errors in the estimation of the Eddington factor of up to 16 % for values of
\mathrm{f}_{g}=0.65
, as shown in figure
5
. Let us now analyze in more detail the dependence on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\delta_{g}
.

The ratio of the Eddington factor
\chi_{g}
in the M1-multigroup model to its gray expression is shown as a function of the radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}
for different values of the group narowness
\delta_{g}
(see figure
6
). Qualitatively, when the group is wide (
\delta_{g}<<1
), the presence of a central “plateau” can be observed within domain
\mathrm{I_{b}}
(see figure
6(a)
). This plateau lies approximately in the interval
\mathcal{T}g\in\left[\sqrt{\delta_{g}}\,;\,1/\sqrt{\delta_{g}}\right]
, which corresponds to radiative temperatures such that
k_{B}\mathrm{T}_{g}\in\left[h\nu_{g-1/2}\,;\,h\nu_{g+1/2}\right]
. As the group becomes narrower, i.e. as
\delta_{g}
increases, the size of the “plateau” decreases until it disappears (see figures
6(b)
and
6(c)
). When the frequencies become nearly identical, two distinct regimes emerge: one at low temperature and one at high temperature. The transition between these regimes occurs at lower radiative temperatures as the frequencies approach one another (
\delta_{g}\rightarrow 1
, see figure
6(d)
). Overall, three main regimes can be identified in figure
6
:

1.

Domain L:
low radiative temperature. In this domain,
\mathcal{T}_{g}<<\sqrt{\delta_{g}}
.
\chi_{g}
depends only on
\mathrm{f}_{g}
. This domain corresponds to that described by Minerbo
[
121
]
;

2.

Domain I:
intermediate radiative temperatures.
\chi_{g}
depends strongly on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
\mathrm{f}_{g}
, and
\delta_{g}
;

3.

Domain H:
high radiative temperature. In this domain,
\mathcal{T}_{g}>>1/\sqrt{\delta_{g}}
. The value of
\chi_{g}
depends only on
\mathrm{f}_{g}
.

(a)

\delta_{g}=10^{-4}
. L, H: asymptotic domains;
\mathrm{I_{a}}
: low-to-intermediate radiative temperatures;
\mathrm{I_{b}}
: domain in which
\chi_{R}
is valid;
\mathrm{I_{c}}
: high-intermediate radiative temperatures.

(b)

\delta_{g}=0.07
. L, H: asymptotic domains;
\mathrm{I_{d}}
: low-to-intermediate radiative temperatures;
\mathrm{I_{e}}
: intermediate-to-high radiative temperatures.

(c)

\delta_{g}=0.3
. L, H: asymptotic domains;
\mathrm{I_{d}}
: low-to-intermediate radiative temperatures;
\mathrm{I_{e}}
: intermediate-to-high radiative temperatures.

(d)

\delta_{g}=0.99
. L, H: asymptotic domains;
\mathrm{I_{f}}
: intermediate radiative temperatures.

Figure 6
:
Ratio between the Eddington factor of the M1-multigroup model,
\chi_{g}
, and that of the M1-gray model,
\chi_{R}
, for a reduced flux
\mathrm{f}_{g}=0.65
, at different values of the group finesse
\delta_{g}
.

The intermediate domain I, based on the group narrowness, can be further subdivided into three cases:

1.

Case
\bm{\delta_{g}\rightarrow 0}
:
wide group. Three intermediate domains can be identified (see figure
6(a)
):

-

\bm{\mathrm{I_{a}}}
:

\chi_{g}
depends strongly on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\mathrm{f}_{g}
;

-

\bm{\mathrm{I_{b}}}
:
corresponds to the domain in which the Eddington factor of the M1-gray model is valid. This domain is located approximately in the interval
\mathcal{T}_{g}\in\left[\sqrt{\delta_{g}}\,;\,1/\sqrt{\delta_{g}}\right]
, and its width is therefore linked to
\delta_{g}
, i.e. it decreases as
\delta_{g}
increases;

-

\bm{\mathrm{I_{c}}}
:

\chi_{g}
depends strongly on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\mathrm{f}_{g}
.

2.

Intermediate
\bm{\delta_{g}}
:
in domains
\mathrm{I_{d}}
and
\mathrm{I_{e}}
,
\chi_{g}
depends on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
,
\mathrm{f}_{g}
, and
\delta_{g}
(see figures
6(b)
and
6(c)
);

3.

Case
\bm{\delta_{g}\rightarrow 1}
:
narrow group. In domain
\mathrm{I_{f}}
,
\chi_{g}
is observed to depend only on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
and
\mathrm{f}_{g}
.

Figure 7
:
Evolution of the ratio between the Eddington factor of the M1-multigroup model,
\chi_{g}
, and that of the M1-gray model,
\chi_{R}
, as a function of
\delta_{g}
, illustrating the shift of the curve as
\delta_{g}
approaches 1.

In the case
\delta_{g}\rightarrow 1
, figure
7
shows the curve of the Eddington factor
\chi_{g}
as a function of the dimensionless radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}
for values of the group narrowness
\delta_{g}
close to 1. The shape of the curve remains unchanged but is shifted toward lower radiative temperatures. In order to understand this behavior, one can seek an approximate expression for the Eddington factor at very high radiative temperatures
\mathcal{T}_{g}
. Let us assume that the dimensionless Lagrange multiplier
\widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}
satisfies
\widetilde{\alpha_{0,g}}<<\sqrt{\delta_{g}}
(i.e.
\alpha_{0,g}<<k_{B}/(h\nu_{g+1/2})
). The specific intensity in group
g
can then be written as:

\mathcal{I}_{\nu,g}(\mu)\approx\frac{2k_{B}}{c^{2}}\frac{\nu^{2}}{\alpha_{0,g}(1+\beta_{g}\mu)}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(75)

which allows the following expressions for the radiative temperature, the reduced flux, and the Eddington factor to be derived:

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{g}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{5h^{3}}{\pi^{4}k_{B}^{3}}\frac{\nu_{g+1/2}^{3}-\nu_{g-1/2}^{3}}{\alpha_{0,g}}\frac{\textnormal{arctanh}(\beta_{g})}{\beta_{g}}\right)^{1/4}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(76)

\displaystyle\mathrm{f}_{g}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\textnormal{arctanh}(\beta_{g})-\beta_{g}}{\beta_{g}\textnormal{arctanh}(\beta_{g})}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(77)

\displaystyle\chi_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\textnormal{arctanh}(\beta_{g})-\beta_{g}}{\beta_{g}^{2}\textnormal{arctanh}(\beta_{g})}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(78)

Equations (
77
) and (
78
) show that, in this case, the Eddington factor depends only on the reduced flux
\mathrm{f}_{g}
and makes it possible to recover the correct expression for domain H. Since
\alpha_{0,g}<<k_{B}/(h\nu_{g+1/2})
, one can derive a condition on
\mathcal{T}_{g}
that guarantees the validity of this approximation:

\mathrm{T}_{g}>>\frac{h\nu_{g+1/2}}{\pi k_{B}}\left(5(1-\delta_{g}^{3})\frac{\textnormal{arctanh}(\beta_{g})}{\beta_{g}}\right)^{1/4}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

Since
\textnormal{arctanh}(\beta_{g})/\beta_{g}\geq 1
for
\beta_{g}\in\left]-1\,;\,1\right[
, this inequality can be rewritten in terms of
\mathcal{T}_{g}
as:

\mathcal{T}_{g}>>\frac{(1-\delta_{g}^{3})^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\delta_{g}}}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(79)

This inequality shows that the expression of the Eddington factor in domain H extends to progressively lower radiative temperatures. This explains the behavior observed in figure
7
.

These observations apply in the general case where
\nu_{g-1/2}>0
and
\nu_{g+1/2}<+\infty
. However, if
\nu_{g-1/2}=0
or
\nu_{g+1/2}=+\infty
, the group narrowness
\delta_{g}
can no longer be defined, nor can the dimensionless radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}
. Let us now examine what happens in these two cases.

❖ Case
\bm{\nu_{g-1/2}=0}
and
\bm{\nu_{g+1/2}<+\infty}

Figure 8
:
Ratio between the Eddington factor of the M1-multigroup model,
\chi_{g}
, and that of the M1-gray model,
\chi_{R}
, for
\mathrm{f}_{g}=0.65
and
\nu_{g-1/2}=0
.
\mathrm{I_{b}}
: domain in which
\chi_{R}
is valid;
\mathrm{I_{c}}
: intermediate-to-high radiative temperatures; H: asymptotically high radiative temperatures.

In the case where
\nu_{g-1/2}=0
, one can perform the change of variable
\tilde{\nu}=\nu/\nu_{g+1/2}
, which allows the radiative temperature, the reduced flux, and the Eddington factor to be expressed as:

\displaystyle\frac{k_{B}\mathrm{T}_{g}}{h\nu_{g+1/2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{15}{\pi^{4}}\int\nolimits_{0}^{1}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \overline{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}\right)^{1/4}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(80)

\displaystyle\mathrm{f}_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\nolimits_{0}^{1}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \overline{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}{\int\nolimits_{0}^{1}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \overline{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(81)

\displaystyle\chi_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\nolimits_{0}^{1}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu^{2}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \overline{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}{\int\nolimits_{0}^{1}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \overline{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(82)

where
\overline{\alpha_{0,g}}=h\nu_{g+1/2}\alpha_{0,g}/k_{B}
is the dimensionless Lagrange multiplier, defined using the frequency
\nu_{g+1/2}
. Using these expressions, one can introduce the dimensionless radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}=k_{B}\mathrm{T}_{g}/(h\nu_{g+1/2})
, which must be used in this case. This temperature can also be defined in the general case where
\nu_{g-1/2}>0
and
\nu_{g+1/2}<+\infty
, and it is related to the radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}
by the relation
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}=\mathcal{T}_{g}\sqrt{\delta_{g}}
.

In this context, one can deduce that the Eddington factor depends only on two variables: the reduced flux
\mathrm{f}_{g}
and the dimensionless radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}
. The evolution of the Eddington factor as a function of the radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}
is shown in figure
8
. In this figure, one can identify domains
\mathrm{I_{b}}
,
\mathrm{I_{c}}
, and H, previously observed in the case of a wide group (
\delta_{g}<<1
). The only difference in the computation of the Eddington factor in this case lies in the choice of the dimensionless radiative temperature used.

❖ Case
\bm{\nu_{g-1/2}>0}
and
\bm{\nu_{g+1/2}=+\infty}

Figure 9
:
Ratio between the Eddington factor of the M1-multigroup model,
\chi_{g}
, and that of the M1-gray model,
\chi_{R}
, for
\mathrm{f}_{g}=0.65
and
\nu_{g+1/2}\rightarrow\infty
. L: asymptotically low radiative temperatures;
\mathrm{I_{a}}
: low-to-intermediate radiative temperatures;
\mathrm{I_{b}}
: domain in which
\chi_{R}
is valid.

In the case where
\nu_{g+1/2}=+\infty
, one can instead perform the change of variable
\tilde{\nu}=\nu/\nu_{g-1/2}
, which allows the radiative temperature, the reduced flux, and the Eddington factor to be expressed as:

\displaystyle\frac{k_{B}\mathrm{T}_{g}}{h\nu_{g-1/2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{15}{\pi^{4}}\int\nolimits_{1}^{+\infty}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widehat{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}\right)^{1/4}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(83)

\displaystyle\mathrm{f}_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\nolimits_{1}^{+\infty}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widehat{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}{\int\nolimits_{1}^{+\infty}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widehat{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(84)

\displaystyle\chi_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int\nolimits_{1}^{+\infty}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\mu^{2}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widehat{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}{\int\nolimits_{1}^{+\infty}\int\nolimits_{-1}^{1}\tilde{\nu}^{3}\left[e^{\tilde{\nu}\penalty\ \widehat{\alpha_{0,g}}\penalty\ (1+\beta_{g}\mu)}-1\right]^{-1}\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\mu\penalty\ \mathrm{d}\tilde{\nu}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(85)

where
\widehat{\alpha_{0,g}}=h\nu_{g-1/2}\alpha_{0,g}/k_{B}
is the dimensionless Lagrange multiplier, defined using the frequency
\nu_{g-1/2}
. From these expressions, one can introduce the dimensionless radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g-1/2}}=k_{B}\mathrm{T}_{g}/(h\nu_{g-1/2})
, which must be used in this case. This temperature can also be defined in the general case where
\nu_{g-1/2}>0
and
\nu_{g+1/2}<+\infty
, and it is related to the radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}
by the relation
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g-1/2}}=\mathcal{T}_{g}/\sqrt{\delta_{g}}
.

In this case, one can deduce that the Eddington factor depends only on two variables: the reduced flux
\mathrm{f}_{g}
and the radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g-1/2}}
. The evolution of the Eddington factor as a function of the radiative temperature
\mathcal{T}_{g}^{\nu_{g-1/2}}
is shown in figure
9
. In this figure, one finds domains L,
\mathrm{I_{a}}
, and
\mathrm{I_{b}}
, previously observed in the case of a wide group (
\delta_{g}<<1
). The only difference lies in the choice of the radiative temperature used.

3
Coupling

Up to this point, the radiative model has been formulated within the framework of a fluid at rest. However, in radiation hydrodynamics models, light propagates through a moving fluid, which induces Doppler effects that modify the energy and momentum exchanged between the fluid and the radiation. Two choices of reference frame are then possible to describe the radiative quantities:

1.

The comoving frame
, in which the fluid is locally at rest. In this framework, matter–radiation interactions (emission and absorption) are isotropic, so that the source terms of the radiative transfer equations (
47
) remain unchanged. However, transforming the radiative quantities from the comoving frame to the laboratory frame introduces additional terms into the equations. In particular, advection effects appear in the form of terms such as
\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\overrightarrow{v}\mathrm{E}_{g})
for the radiative energy and
\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\overrightarrow{v}\otimes\overrightarrow{F_{g}})
for the radiative flux
[
120
,
22
,
104
]
. Moreover, the Doppler effect induces a change in photon frequency between the two frames. Within the M1-multigroup model, this implies transfers of energy and momentum between spectral groups, represented by the terms
\overrightarrow{\nabla}\overrightarrow{v}:\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\partial_{\nu}(\nu\mathbbm{P}\nu)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu
and
\overrightarrow{\nabla}\overrightarrow{v}:\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\partial_{\nu}(\nu\mathbbm{Q}_{\nu})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu
. These terms make the solution of the radiative transfer equations in the M1-multigroup model particularly complex
[
167
]
;

2.

The laboratory frame
, in which the fluid is in motion. In this frame, the radiative transfer equations (
47
) retain their usual form, without the introduction of advection terms or energy transfers between spectral groups in the M1-multigroup model. By contrast, the source terms become more complex, as they must include Doppler effects and geometric aberrations
[
119
]
.

In this work, we adopt this second approach by expressing the radiative quantities in the laboratory frame.

3.1
Lorentz transformation

Before proceeding, let us recall the expression of the Lorentz transformation. Consider two reference frames,
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, where
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
moves with velocity
\overrightarrow{v}
relative to
\mathcal{R}
. In special relativity, the transformation of a four-vector
\mathchoice{X^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{X^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{X^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{X^{{{\alpha}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
from the reference frame
\mathcal{R}
to the reference frame
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is expressed as follows:

X^{\prime\beta}=\mathchoice{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}X^{\alpha}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(86)

where
X^{\prime\beta}
is the four-vector in the reference frame
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, and
\mathchoice{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}
is the Lorentz tensor, given by:

\mathchoice{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}{\Lambda^{{{\beta}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}}}}=\begin{bmatrix}\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}\gamma_{u}&-\gamma_{u}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}^{T}\\
\hline\cr&\\
-\gamma_{u}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}&\mathbbm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}+\frac{\gamma_{u}^{2}}{\gamma_{u}+1}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\otimes\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\\
&\\
\end{array}\end{bmatrix}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(87)

with:

\displaystyle\gamma_{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\beta_{u}^{2}}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}=\overrightarrow{v}/c\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\beta_{u}=||\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}||\;\;\mathpunct{.}

The quantity
\gamma_{u}
is called the
Lorentz factor
. Let us first consider the case of the M1-multigroup model. We then define the second-order radiation energy–momentum tensor of group
g
, denoted
\mathbbm{R}_{g}=(R_{g}^{\alpha\beta})_{\alpha,\beta}
, as:

R_{g}^{\alpha\beta}=c^{-1}\int\nolimits_{\nu_{g-1/2}}^{\nu_{g+1/2}}\int\nolimits_{4\pi}I_{\nu}(\overrightarrow{n})n^{\alpha}n^{\beta}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\Omega\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(88)

where
n^{\alpha}=(n^{0},\overrightarrow{n})=(1,\overrightarrow{n})
. This tensor can also be written in the form:

R_{g}^{\alpha\beta}=\begin{bmatrix}\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}\mathrm{E}_{g}&\overrightarrow{F_{g}}^{T}/c\\
\hline\cr&\\
\overrightarrow{F_{g}}/c&\mathbbm{P}_{g}\\
&\\
\end{array}\end{bmatrix}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(89)

To transform this tensor from the comoving frame to the laboratory frame, it is necessary to apply the Lorentz transformation to each contravariant index. Knowing that the laboratory frame moves with velocity
\overrightarrow{v}
relative to the comoving frame, the radiative quantities in the latter, namely
\mathrm{E}_{g}
,
\overrightarrow{F_{g}}
, and
\mathbbm{P}_{g}
, can be expressed as functions of the radiative quantities in the laboratory frame according to the following relations:

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}_{g,0}=

\displaystyle\gamma_{u}^{2}\left[\mathrm{E}_{g}-\frac{2\overrightarrow{v}\cdot\overrightarrow{F_{g}}}{c^{2}}+\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}^{T}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\right]\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(90)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{F_{g,0}}=

\displaystyle\gamma_{u}\left[\overrightarrow{F_{g}}-\left\{\mathbbm{P}_{g}+\gamma_{u}\mathbbm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}\left(\mathrm{E}_{g}-\frac{2\gamma_{u}+1}{\gamma_{u}+1}\frac{\overrightarrow{v}\cdot\overrightarrow{F_{g}}}{c^{2}}+\frac{\gamma_{u}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}^{T}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}}{\gamma_{u}+1}\right)\right\}\overrightarrow{v}\right]\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(91)

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}_{g,0}=

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}_{g}-\gamma_{u}\frac{\overrightarrow{v}\otimes\overrightarrow{F_{g}}+\overrightarrow{F_{g}}\otimes\overrightarrow{v}}{c^{2}}-\gamma_{u}^{2}\left[\frac{\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\otimes\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}+\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\otimes\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\mathbbm{P}_{g}}{\gamma_{u}+1}+\right.

\displaystyle\left.\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\otimes\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\left\{\mathrm{E}_{g}-\frac{2\gamma_{u}}{\gamma_{u}+1}\left(\frac{\overrightarrow{v}\cdot\overrightarrow{F_{g}}}{c^{2}}-\frac{\gamma_{u}}{2}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}^{T}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\right)\right\}\right]\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(92)

Let us now define the four-vector of source terms of the radiative transfer equation as
S_{g}^{\alpha}=(S^{0}_{g},\overrightarrow{S}_{g})
. By applying the Lorentz transformation, the source terms in the laboratory frame can be expressed in terms of those in the comoving frame through the following relations:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{u}\left(S^{0}_{g,0}+\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\cdot\overrightarrow{S_{g,0}}\right)\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(93)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S_{g}}

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{S_{g,0}}+\gamma_{u}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\left(S^{0}_{g,0}+\frac{\gamma_{u}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\cdot\overrightarrow{S_{g,0}}}{\gamma_{u}+1}\right)\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(94)

As we saw in Section
2
.
2
Radiative transfer
, in the comoving frame the source term can be written as:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g,0}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{P,g}(\mathrm{E}_{g,0}-a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T}))\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S_{g,0}}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{R,g}\overrightarrow{F_{g,0}}/c\;\;\mathpunct{,}

where
\kappa_{P,g}
and
\kappa_{R,g}
are the Planck and Rosseland opacities of group
g
in the comoving frame. Using transformations (
93
) and (
94
), the source terms can therefore be rewritten in the laboratory frame as:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{u}\left(\kappa_{P,g}(\mathrm{E}_{g,0}-a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T}))+\kappa_{R,g}\frac{\overrightarrow{v}\cdot\overrightarrow{F}_{g,0}}{c^{2}}\right)\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S_{g}}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{R}\overrightarrow{F}_{g,0}/c+\gamma_{u}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\left(\kappa_{P,g}(\mathrm{E}_{g,0}-a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T}))+\frac{\gamma_{u}\kappa_{R,g}}{\gamma_{u}+1}\frac{\overrightarrow{v}\cdot\overrightarrow{F_{g,0}}}{c^{2}}\right)\;\;\mathpunct{.}

Finally, by rewriting this source term in terms of the radiative quantities expressed in the laboratory frame and using transformations (
90
), (
91
), and (
92
), one obtains the following expression for the source terms in the laboratory frame
[
119
]
:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g}=

\displaystyle\gamma_{u}\left[\left(\gamma_{u}^{2}\left(\kappa_{P,g}-\beta_{u}^{2}\kappa_{R,g}\right)\mathrm{E}_{g}-\kappa_{P,g}a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T})\right)+\right.

\displaystyle\left.\gamma_{u}^{2}\left\{\left((1+\beta_{u}^{2})\kappa_{R,g}-2\kappa_{P,g}\right)\overrightarrow{v}\cdot(c^{-2}\overrightarrow{F_{g}})-\left(\kappa_{R,g}-\kappa_{P,g}\right)\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}^{T}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\right\}\right]\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(95)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S_{g}}=

\displaystyle\gamma_{u}\left[\kappa_{R,g}\overrightarrow{F_{g}}/c-\kappa_{R,g}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}-\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\left\{\left(\gamma_{u}^{2}(\kappa_{R,g}-\kappa_{P,g})\mathrm{E}_{g}+\kappa_{P,g}a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T})\right)-\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\gamma_{u}^{2}(\kappa_{R,g}-\kappa_{P,g})\left(2\overrightarrow{v}\cdot(c^{-2}\overrightarrow{F_{g}})-\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}^{T}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\right)\right\}\right]\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(96)

In this work, we assume that the fluid is non-relativistic (
\beta_{u}<<1
). Therefore, these source terms can be expanded to first order in
\beta
, noting that
\gamma_{u}=1+\mathcal{O}(\beta_{u}^{2})
:

\displaystyle S^{0}_{g}=

\displaystyle\kappa_{P,g}\left(\mathrm{E}_{g}-a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T})\right)+(\kappa_{R,g}-2\kappa_{P,g})\overrightarrow{v}\cdot(c^{-2}\overrightarrow{F_{g}})\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(97)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S_{g}}=

\displaystyle\kappa_{R,g}\overrightarrow{F_{g}}/c-\kappa_{R,g}\mathbbm{P}_{g}\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}-\overrightarrow{\beta_{u}}\left\{(\kappa_{R,g}-\kappa_{P,g})\mathrm{E}_{g}+\kappa_{P,g}a_{R}\theta_{g}^{4}(\mathrm{T})\right\}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(98)

3.2
Radiative hydrodynamics equations

We can now combine the hydrodynamics equations (
8
) and the radiative transfer equations into a coupled system, forming what is known as the
radiation hydrodynamics equations
. These equations describe the evolution of a fluid interacting with radiation.

Let us first present these equations within the framework of the M1-multigroup model for the description of radiation. In this context, the source terms
cS^{0}_{g}
and
\overrightarrow{S_{g}}
introduced previously (equations (
97
) and (
98
)) represent, respectively, the exchanges of energy and momentum between the fluid and the radiation contained in each frequency group. These exchanges are taken into account by adding the total contribution of these interactions as source terms on the right-hand side of the hydrodynamics equations (
8
) and by incorporating them into the radiative transfer equations (
47
), formulated in the laboratory frame. One thus obtains the complete system of radiation hydrodynamics equations:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\partial_{t}\rho+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v})&=0\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\overrightarrow{v})+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v}\otimes\overrightarrow{v}+p\mathbbm{I}_{\mathrm{d}})&=\sum_{g=1}^{\mathcal{G}}\overrightarrow{S_{g}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot((\mathrm{E}+p)\overrightarrow{v})&=\sum_{g=1}^{\mathcal{G}}cS^{0}_{g}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}_{g}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\overrightarrow{F_{g}}&=-cS^{0}_{g},&\forall\penalty\ g\penalty\ \in\penalty\ \llbracket 1,\penalty\ \mathcal{G}\rrbracket\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}(c^{-2}\overrightarrow{F_{g}})+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\mathbbm{P}_{g}&=-\overrightarrow{S_{g}},&\forall\penalty\ g\penalty\ \in\penalty\ \llbracket 1,\penalty\ \mathcal{G}\rrbracket\;\;\mathpunct{.}\end{array}\right.

(99)

In the particular case of the M1-gray model, the radiation hydrodynamics equations take a simpler form:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\partial_{t}\rho+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v})&=0\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\overrightarrow{v})+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v}\otimes\overrightarrow{v}+p\mathbbm{I}_{\mathrm{d}})&=\overrightarrow{S}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot((\mathrm{E}+p)\overrightarrow{v})&=cS^{0}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}_{R}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\overrightarrow{F_{R}}&=-cS^{0}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}(c^{-2}\overrightarrow{F_{R}})+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\mathbbm{P}_{R}&=-\overrightarrow{S}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\end{array}\right.

(100)

where
S^{0}
and
\overrightarrow{S}
are the source terms of the single group considered, integrated over the entire electromagnetic spectrum.

3.3
Asymptotic cases

Now that a general radiation hydrodynamics model has been established, it is essential to verify whether it is able to recover the well-known asymptotic limits: the optically thin regime and the optically thick regime. To this end, one can first identify several characteristic lengths defined from the source terms (
97
) and (
98
):

\displaystyle\lambda_{th}

\displaystyle=1/\max_{\nu}(\kappa_{\nu})\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\lambda_{tr}

\displaystyle=1/\max_{\nu}(\chi_{\nu})\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\lambda_{dyn,1}

\displaystyle=1/\max_{\nu}(|\chi_{\nu}-2\kappa_{\nu}|)\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\lambda_{dyn,2}

\displaystyle=1/\max_{\nu}(\sigma_{\nu})\;\;\mathpunct{.}

These characteristic lengths, in turn, allow four associated characteristic times to be defined:

\displaystyle\tau_{th}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{th}}{c}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(temps de thermalisation)

\displaystyle\tau_{tr}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{tr}}{c}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(temps de transport)

\displaystyle\tau_{dyn,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{dyn,1}}{\beta_{u}c}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(temps dynamique 1)

\displaystyle\tau_{dyn,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda_{dyn,2}}{\beta_{u}c}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

(temps dynamique 2)

These characteristic times play a key role in the dynamics of radiation–matter interactions:

•

\tau_{th}
corresponds to the time required for the gas to reach thermal equilibrium with the radiation,

•

\tau_{tr}
represents the characteristic timescale of radiative transport,

•

the times
\tau_{dyn,1}
and
\tau_{dyn,2}
quantify the dynamical interaction between radiation and matter in the presence of nonzero fluid velocities.

Three important observations can be made:

1.

Hierarchy of timescales:

In the non-relativistic framework (
\beta_{u}\ll 1
), one observes that
\tau_{th},\tau_{tr}\ll\tau_{dyn,1},\tau_{dyn,2}
. This means that radiation has time to thermalize and to be transported before dynamical effects associated with fluid motion (Doppler effect, relativistic distortions) become significant. Thus, on short timescales, radiation behaves as if it were in a rest frame;

2.

Contribution of higher-order terms in
\bm{\beta_{u}}
:

If the expansion of the source terms
S_{0}
and
\overrightarrow{S}_{g}
had not been truncated at first order in
\beta_{u}
, additional contributions would appear, associated with characteristic times of the form
\tau_{n}=\lambda/(\beta_{u}^{n}c)
with
n\geq 2
. However, as long as one considers situations in which the characteristic durations are sufficiently short, the impact of these terms remains negligible on the global dynamics of the fluid and the radiation;

3.

Case where scattering is neglected (
\bm{\sigma_{\nu}\approx 0}
):

By neglecting photon scattering and assuming that the absorption coefficient is independent of photon frequency (
\kappa_{\nu}\approx\chi_{\nu}=\kappa
), the characteristic times simplify:

\displaystyle\tau_{tr}\approx\tau_{th}=\tau=\frac{1}{\kappa c}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\tau_{dyn,1}\approx\frac{\tau}{\beta_{u}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\tau_{dyn,2}

\displaystyle\to+\infty.

In this case, the behavior of the system depends on the ratio between the characteristic time
t
of the situation under consideration and the timescales defined above
[
93
]
:

(a)

\bm{t<<\tau}
: Optically thin regime (free streaming)

Radiation propagates freely with negligible interaction with matter. This regime corresponds to the optically thin case;

(b)

\bm{t>>\tau}
and
\bm{t<<\tau/\beta_{u}}
: Static diffusion regime

Radiation is in thermal equilibrium with matter, but its dynamical interaction with the fluid remains negligible;

(c)

\bm{t>>\tau}
,
\bm{t>>\tau/\beta_{u}}
, and
\bm{t<<\tau/\beta_{u}^{2}}
: Dynamic diffusion regime

The interaction between radiation and gas becomes dominant, and radiative transport is essentially governed by fluid advection. This regime remains within the validity limits of the model developed here.

Thus, depending on the characteristic timescale considered, the physical situation differs, and different approximations may be employed. Let us now present the two main existing approximations used to describe radiation.

❖ Optically thin case

In the interstellar medium, matter is very often optically thin because of the low densities and temperatures that prevail there. This implies weak interactions between radiation and matter, which translates into small opacities
\kappa_{P}
and
\kappa_{R}
. Consequently, the characteristic thermalization and transport times, denoted
\tau_{th}
and
\tau_{tr}
, respectively, are very long. In other words, radiation takes a long time to exchange energy with the surrounding matter and to propagate through the medium. It is therefore reasonable to assume that, in this regime, the influence of radiation on the fluid dynamics remains negligible.

Let us now consider a situation in which, initially, radiation is in radiative equilibrium with matter, which implies that the radiative flux is nearly zero (
\overrightarrow{F_{R}}\approx 0
) and that the radiative energy satisfies
\mathrm{E}_{R}=a_{R}\mathrm{T}_{i}^{4}
, where
\mathrm{T}_{i}
is the initial temperature of the fluid. Suppose that, at some time, the fluid is heated, reaching a temperature
\mathrm{T_{f}}
such that
\mathrm{T_{f}}^{4}\gg\mathrm{T}_{i}^{4}
. In this case, the source terms describing matter–radiation interactions, given by expressions (
97
) and (
98
), can be approximated by:

\displaystyle S^{0}\approx

\displaystyle-\kappa_{P}(\rho,\mathrm{T})a_{R}\mathrm{T}^{4}=-\Lambda(\rho,\mathrm{T})\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(101)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{S}\penalty\ \approx

\displaystyle 0\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(102)

where
\kappa_{P}
is the Planck mean opacity, defined by equation (
38
), taking
\nu_{g-1/2}=0
and
\nu_{g+1/2}=+\infty
. One recognizes here the typical form of the cooling function
\Lambda(\rho,\mathrm{T})
, which depends only on the gas density
\rho
and its temperature
\mathrm{T}
. Assuming that the Planck opacity can be described by a power law in
\rho
and
\mathrm{T}
, this cooling function can be written in the general form:

\Lambda(\rho,\mathrm{T})\approx\Lambda_{0}\rho^{\alpha}\mathrm{T}^{\beta}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(103)

where
\Lambda_{0}
is a constant that depends in particular on the radiation constant
a_{R}
, while
\alpha
and
\beta
are exponents characterizing the physical processes involved. For instance, to model bremsstrahlung cooling in a hot interstellar medium (
\mathrm{T}>10^{7}
K
), one would choose
\alpha=2
and
\beta=0.5
. By contrast, for cooling via inverse Compton scattering of electrons, the commonly used values are
\alpha=1
and
\beta=1
.

Since we assume that the gas follows the ideal-gas equation of state (
10
), it is also possible to express this cooling function in terms of the thermal pressure
p
and the density
\rho
as follows:

\Lambda(\rho,p)\approx\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\rho^{\epsilon}p^{\zeta}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(104)

where
\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}
is a constant related to
\Lambda_{0}
through
\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}=\Lambda_{0}\mu m_{H}/k_{B}
. The exponents
\epsilon
and
\zeta
are related to
\alpha
and
\beta
by
\epsilon=\alpha-\beta
and
\zeta=\beta
. In this framework, the simplified radiation hydrodynamics equations take the form:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\partial_{t}\rho+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v})&=0\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\overrightarrow{v})+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v}\otimes\overrightarrow{v}+p)&=0\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot((\mathrm{E}+p)\overrightarrow{v})&=-\Lambda(\rho,p)\;\;\mathpunct{.}\end{array}\right.

(105)

This formulation is widely used because of its numerical simplicity: by avoiding the explicit resolution of the radiative transfer equations, it significantly reduces both the complexity of the calculations and the computational cost.

❖ Optically thick case

Conversely, in dense regions such as stellar interiors, the medium is highly opaque and interacts strongly with radiation. In this regime, radiation is in near-equilibrium with matter, since the characteristic thermalization and transport times are very short compared with the dynamical timescales under consideration (
t\gg\tau_{th},\tau_{tr}
). One can then assume that radiative equilibrium is reached instantaneously and express the radiative pressure as well as the radiative flux as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbbm{P}_{R,0}=\frac{1}{3}\mathbbm{I}_{\mathrm{d}}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{F}_{R,0}=

\displaystyle-\frac{c}{3\kappa_{R}}\overrightarrow{\nabla}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

where
\kappa_{R}
is the Rosseland opacity defined by (
39
), with
\nu_{g-1/2}=0
and
\nu_{g+1/2}=+\infty
. These expressions reflect the fact that, in an optically thick medium, radiation interacts strongly with matter and propagates by diffusion. The radiative pressure is isotropic, while the radiative flux follows a diffusion law, proportional to the gradient of the radiative energy, with a diffusion coefficient given by
c/(3\kappa_{R})
.

Using these approximations, it becomes possible to retain only the radiative energy equation to describe radiation. Keeping only terms of order
\mathcal{O}(v/c)
as well as certain terms of order
\mathcal{O}((v/c)^{2})
, and expressing the radiative quantities in the comoving frame, the radiation hydrodynamics equations can be rewritten in the form
[
144
]
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llll}\partial_{t}\rho+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v})&=0\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}(\rho\overrightarrow{v})+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overrightarrow{v}\otimes\overrightarrow{v}+p)&=-\frac{1}{3}\overrightarrow{\nabla}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot((\mathrm{E}+p)\overrightarrow{v})&=c\kappa_{P}(\mathrm{E}_{R,0}-a_{R}\mathrm{T}^{4})+\frac{1}{3}\overrightarrow{v}\cdot\overrightarrow{\nabla}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}\;\;\mathpunct{,}\\
\partial_{t}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}+\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{4}{3}\overrightarrow{v}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}\right)&=\overrightarrow{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{c}{3\kappa_{R}}\overrightarrow{\nabla}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}\right)-c\kappa_{P}(\mathrm{E}_{R,0}-a_{R}\mathrm{T}^{4})-\frac{1}{3}\overrightarrow{v}\cdot\overrightarrow{\nabla}\mathrm{E}_{R,0}\;\;\mathpunct{.}\end{array}\right.

(106)

Compared with the complete system (
100
), this simplified model contains only four equations instead of five, which greatly facilitates its numerical solution. It thus avoids the explicit treatment of detailed radiative transfer, while still capturing the essential interactions between matter and radiation in an optically thick medium. This formalism describes both the static diffusion regime and the dynamic diffusion regime, as introduced by Krumholz
[
93
]
. However, it does not allow one to treat regions where radiative equilibrium is locally broken, as is the case in radiative shocks (see Section
8
.
8
Introduction to radiative shocks
).

4
Synthesis

In this chapter, I have presented the model used to simulate radiation hydrodynamics. This model relies on several simplifying assumptions:

•

the gas is ideal, inviscid, and without thermal diffusion,

•

it is assumed to be non-ionized,

•

gravitational effects are neglected,

•

the gas and the radiation are in LTE,

•

photon scattering is assumed to be elastic and isotropic, with an isotropic scattering coefficient,

•

the fluid is treated within the non-relativistic framework.

These assumptions make it possible to derive the radiation hydrodynamics equations based on the M1-multigroup model for the description of radiative transfer. Since the Eddington factor associated with this model does not admit an analytical expression but can be determined numerically, I have studied its dependencies and variations in order to identify its main characteristics. The next chapter introduces AIand describes the work I have carried out to leverage it with the aim of improving the efficiency of radiation hydrodynamics simulations.

Chapter 3
Calculation of the Eddington factor with Artificial Intelligence

T
he HADEScode
[
126
,
114
,
117
]
, developed to solve the radiation hydrodynamics equations, models the interaction between radiation and matter outside radiative equilibrium. It was designed to limit approximations in the treatment of the radiation field as much as possible. Two radiative transfer models are implemented in the code: the M1-gray model and the M1-multigroup model, which make it possible to solve the equations introduced in the previous chapter using classical spatial and temporal discretization methods. We first present the numerical methods used in this code and rewrite the radiation hydrodynamics equations in the following form:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{q}_{h}+\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{q}_{h})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}_{h}\quad\text{(hydrodynamics)}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\mathbf{q}_{r}+\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{q}_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{s}_{r}\quad\text{(radiative transfer)}

The algorithm used in HADESto solve the radiation hydrodynamics equations consists of two main steps
[
126
]
:

1.

Update of the radiative quantities
\mathbf{q}_{r}
over two time steps
\Delta t
,

2.

Update of the hydrodynamic quantities
\mathbf{q}_{h}
over two time steps
\Delta t
.

Each of these steps is further decomposed into three substeps:

1.

Resolution of the Ordinary Differential Equation (ODE)
\partial_{t}\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{s}
over a time step
\Delta t/2
(explicit scheme for hydrodynamics, implicit scheme for radiation),

2.

Resolution of the Partial Differential Equations (PDE)
\partial_{t}\mathbf{q}+\nabla\cdot\mathbf{F}(\mathbf{q})=0
over a time step
\Delta t
(for instance, an Harten-Lax-van Leer-Einfeld (HLLE)Riemann solver for hydrodynamics and an Harten-Lax-van Leer (HLL)Riemann solver for radiation),

3.

Resolution of the ODE
\partial_{t}\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{s}
over a time step
\Delta t/2
(explicit scheme for hydrodynamics, implicit scheme for radiation).

This algorithm guarantees second-order accuracy in the solution of the radiation hydrodynamics equations. However, two major limitations remain:

1.

Computation of the Eddington factor in the M1-multigroup model

In this model, the Eddington factor does not admit an explicit analytical expression. The code therefore relies on numerical root-finding algorithms to estimate it accurately. Although these methods provide precise values of the Eddington factor, they are particularly expensive in terms of computational cost;

2.

Courant–Friedrichs–Lewy (CFL) condition

To ensure numerical stability when solving the radiation hydrodynamics equations, the time step
\Delta t
and the spatial step
\Delta x
must satisfy a CFL-type condition:
c\Delta t/\Delta x\leq 1
, where
c
denotes the speed of light. This constraint forces the use of very small time steps, which significantly increases the computational cost of the simulations.

In order to overcome these limitations and accelerate the simulations, I explored the potential contribution of AI. This technology has attracted growing interest and is experiencing rapid development in fields as diverse as medicine, robotics, and space exploration. More recently, it has been integrated into numerical simulation workflows, giving rise to an emerging research field known as NeuHPC
[
83
]
. This field develops solutions along two main directions:

1.

Inverse modeling:
The goal is to discover the analytical or differential equations underlying the data. This approach includes symbolic regression
5
5

5

Python library:
https://github.com/MilesCranmer/PySR
, which aims at identifying explicit analytical expressions; Julia library:
https://github.com/MilesCranmer/SymbolicRegression.jl

[
5
]
, as well as the use of PINNs to infer the differential equations governing the observed phenomena;

2.

Simulation:
AIis used to solve known differential equations, either by fully integrating them using machine-learning techniques or by combining traditional numerical methods with AI-based approaches.

In this work, I focus more specifically on the second direction, with the aim of developing more efficient methods to solve the radiation hydrodynamics equations. Three broad categories of neural-network applications in simulation can be identified:

1.

AIsolver:
These methods consist in using AIto solve differential equations end to end. For instance, PINNs approximate the solution by treating the neural network as a function that approximates the solution of the underlying differential equations
[
137
,
78
,
36
]
. Another strategy relies on neural operators, which learn to map the solution at time
t
to that at time
t+\Delta t
, using architectures such as Graph Neural Networks (GNNs)
[
146
,
3
,
21
,
71
]
or Fourier Neural Operators (FNOs)
[
100
,
101
]
;

2.

Closure relations:
In this case, neural networks are used to determine or improve the closure relations of the equations. This approach can be applied within simplified models, for example for turbulence modeled by the RANS or LES equations
[
94
,
158
,
29
]
, or to establish an unknown closure relation
[
67
]
;

3.

Error correction:
This approach consists in training a neural network on data generated by very high-resolution simulations and then using it to correct the errors made in low-resolution simulations. This procedure makes it possible to drastically reduce computational cost while maintaining high accuracy in the results
[
89
]
.

In this work, I explored two complementary approaches to reduce the computational cost of radiation hydrodynamics simulations. The first consists in developing an AI-based method to efficiently estimate the Eddington factor involved in the closure relation of the M1-multigroup model (see Section
6
.
6
Eddington factor in the M1-multigroup model
). The second aims at investigating the feasibility of a neural solver, based on PINNs, capable of extrapolating a simulation from the initial data of an existing simulation, while relying directly on the radiation hydrodynamics equations (see Chapter
5
.
Chapter 5
Extrapolation of radiative shock simulations with Physics-Informed Neural Networks
).

In the present chapter, I will first present the main AItechniques used for training neural networks, before detailing the proposed method for accelerating the evaluation of the Eddington factor.

5
Steps to generate a functional neural network

When addressing a problem in AI, several fundamental questions must be posed and resolved during the development of the method.

•

Which data should be used?

This involves determining the nature, quality, format, and quantity of the data required to train the network.

•

How should the model error be measured (cost function)?

The choice of the loss function (evaluation of the neural network’s performance) is essential to guide the learning process.

•

Which optimization algorithm should be used?

The optimizer adjusts the parameters of the neural network in order to minimize the loss function.

•

Which neural network architecture should be adopted?

A neural network can be viewed as a more or less complex function, characterized by a large number of adjustable parameters. The choice of its architecture—that is, the way in which the computational units are organized and connected—determines the form of this function. Among the common architectures in AI, one finds in particular Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP)s, Generative Adversarial Networks (GAN)s, or Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM)s. This decision depends on the type of relationship to be modeled, the complexity of the problem, as well as constraints in terms of computational resources and available data.

•

Should a particular strategy be adopted to promote generalization?

This involves helping the neural network produce accurate predictions on configurations it has not seen during training.

These choices are not independent and must be adjusted iteratively. An initial combination of assumptions is tested and then modified in light of the results obtained, until convergence toward a solution deemed satisfactory. This process is illustrated in figure
10
in the form of a decision loop. Indeed, answering one question (for example,
which data should be used?
) makes it possible to address the next one (for example,
which architecture should be adopted?
), and so on, up to the evaluation of the effectiveness of the choices made. This cycle is repeated several times with different combinations before retaining the most successful trial.

In this case, since the objective is to reduce the computational cost of numerical simulations, I imposed a strong constraint on the architecture: I opted for MLPs, one of the simplest architectures, yet sufficiently expressive to approximate complex nonlinear relationships while remaining computationally inexpensive.

In this section, I will first introduce MLPs, before detailing the possible choices regarding the loss function, the optimization strategy, as well as techniques aimed at improving generalization capability.

Figure 10
:
Diagram illustrating the reasoning process involved in developing an artificial intelligence strategy.

5.1
Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLP)

An MLPcan be viewed as a complex mathematical function, having a large number of adjustable parameters depending on the problem being addressed. It is composed of elementary units called
perceptrons
(see figure
11
), whose formulation was first introduced by Rosenblatt (1958)
[
141
]
. Each perceptron receives
n
inputs
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
, …,
x_{n}
, associated with
weights

w_{1}
,
w_{2}
, …,
w_{n}
, as well as a
bias

b
. It produces an output
y
, defined by the following expression:

y=\sigma\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}+b\right)\;\;\mathpunct{,}

Figure 11
:
Diagram of a perceptron

where
\sigma
denotes an
activation function
, which may be linear or nonlinear. The introduction of nonlinearity allows the perceptron to capture complex behaviors and to model nonlinear phenomena. Several activation functions are commonly used:

•

the identity function (see figure
12(a)
)

\sigma(x)=x\in\mathbb{R}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

•

the sigmoid function (see figure
12(b)
)

\sigma(x)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}\in\left[0\,;\,1\right]\;\;\mathpunct{,}

•

the hyperbolic tangent function (see figure
12(c)
)

\sigma(x)=\tanh(x)=\frac{1-e^{-x}}{1+e^{-x}}\in\left[-1\,;\,1\right]\;\;\mathpunct{,}

•

the arctangent function (see figure
12(d)
)

\sigma(x)=\mathrm{Arctan}(x)\in\left[-\pi/2\,;\,\pi/2\right]\;\;\mathpunct{,}

•

the Rectified Linear Unit (ReLU) (see figure
12(e)
)

\sigma(x)=\begin{cases}0\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ x<0\\
x\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{if}\penalty\ \penalty\ x\geq 0\end{cases}\in\mathbb{R^{+}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

•

the soft rectified linear unit (SoftPlus) (see figure
12(f)
)

\sigma(x)=\ln(1+e^{-x})\in\mathbb{R^{+}}\;\;\mathpunct{,}

(a)

Identity function

(b)

Sigmoid function

(c)

Hyperbolic tangent function

(d)

Arctangent function

(e)

ReLU function

(f)

SoftPlus function

Figure 12
:
Examples of activation functions that can be used in neural networks.

Although most of these activation functions are nonlinear, they generally do not allow the representation of discontinuities. Some alternative variants have nevertheless been proposed, notably by DellaSanta (2023)
[
38
]
, which explicitly introduce discontinuities into the activation function in order to endow the network with specific properties. The adoption of such functions, however, requires particular care, both from a theoretical and a numerical standpoint, due to the consequences they may have on training stability and the local differentiability of the model.

However, a single perceptron is a relatively simple function with limited capacity to represent complex functions. To overcome this limitation, multiple perceptrons are grouped together to form a
layer
. By subsequently stacking several layers of perceptrons, one obtains what is known as a neural network of type MLP. Three types of layers are generally distinguished:

1.

The input layer
, which receives the input data and transmits it to the network;

2.

The hidden layers
, located between the input layer and the output layer, which extract increasingly abstract and complex features from the data. More than one hidden layer may be used;

3.

The output layer
, which produces the final results of the network, depending on the task (classification, regression, etc.).

Figure
13
illustrates an example of an MLPneural network, consisting of an input layer with five neurons, an output layer with three neurons, and two hidden layers each containing four neurons. An MLPneural network has a large number of parameters (weights and biases of the perceptrons) that must be adjusted. Hornik (1989)
[
72
]
showed that this type of network is a universal function approximator, meaning that it can theoretically approximate any function, provided that a sufficiently large architecture and an appropriate parameter configuration are available. The output produced by this network, referred to as the
prediction
, depends directly on these parameters. The process of optimizing the parameters to achieve the best possible performance on a given task constitutes the essence of neural network
training
.

Figure 13
:
Example of an MLPwith five inputs and three outputs. The network consists of an input layer, two hidden layers, and an output layer. The red circles represent the neurons (or perceptrons), and
\sigma
denotes the activation function applied to each of them. The same activation function may be used for all neurons, or different functions may be employed depending on the specific needs of the model.

5.2
Training

Training consists in optimizing the weights and biases of the perceptrons in the neural network in order to minimize the error made by the network. First, one must select the data used to train the neural network. In general, these data are divided into three datasets:

1.

The training set
, which contains labeled examples used to train the neural network;

2.

The validation set
, which is used to evaluate the generalization capability of the model and to tune the hyperparameters in order to avoid overfitting
6
6

6

See Section
5.3
.
5.3
Underfitting and overfitting
for the definition of overfitting.
;

3.

The test set
, which is completely independent of the previous datasets and is used to assess the final performance of the model on unseen data.

To evaluate the overall quality of the predictions produced by the neural network, a
cost function
is introduced. This function measures the global error made by the model. Let us assume that the dataset contains
N
samples, that
y^{(i)}
denotes the expected output of the neural network for the input
x^{(i)}
, and that
h_{\bm{\theta}}(x^{(i)})
represents the prediction produced by the network for the same input.

Several cost functions are commonly used in artificial intelligence; some of them are presented below.

•

Mean Squared Error (MSE)

MSE is the most commonly used option. It strongly penalizes large errors, which makes it sensitive to outliers.

\mathcal{L}(Y,h_{\bm{\theta}}(X))=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[h_{\bm{\theta}}(x^{(i)})-y^{(i)}\right]^{2}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

•

Mean Absolute Error (MAE)

Unlike MSE, MAE penalizes errors linearly, which makes it more robust to outliers. It is frequently used in regression tasks.

\mathcal{L}(Y,h_{\bm{\theta}}(X))=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left|h_{\bm{\theta}}(x^{(i)})-y^{(i)}\right|\;\;\mathpunct{.}

•

Logarithmic Mean Squared Error (LMSE)

This cost function is particularly well suited when the data are strictly positive and when minimizing relative error is more important than minimizing absolute error. It is often used in problems where target values span several orders of magnitude.

\mathcal{L}(Y,h_{\bm{\theta}}(X))=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\ln\left(h_{\bm{\theta}}(x^{(i)})\right)-\ln\left(y^{(i)}\right)\right]^{2}\;\;\mathpunct{.}

•

Binary Cross-Entropy

Used in binary classification tasks, this function measures the divergence between the predicted probabilities and the true labels
y^{(i)}
, which are assumed to belong to
\{0,1\}
. It requires a network whose outputs lie in the interval
\left[0\,;\,1\right]
.
